A theory of Automated Market Makers in DeFi
Massimo Bartoletti ORCID:0000-0003-3796-9774, James Hsin-yu Chiang ORCID:0000-0002-5126-9494, Alberto Lluch-Lafuente ORCID:0000-0001-7405-0818

Abstract
. Automated market makers (AMMs) are one of the most prominent decentralized finance (DeFi) applications. AMMs allow users to trade different types of crypto-tokens, without the need to find a counter-party. There are several implementations and models for AMMs, featuring a variety of sophisticated economic mechanisms. We present a theory of AMMs. The core of our theory is an abstract operational model of the interactions between users and AMMs, which can be concretised by instantiating the economic mechanisms. We exploit our theory to formally prove a set of fundamental properties of AMMs, characterizing both structural and economic aspects. We do this by abstracting from the actual economic mechanisms used in implementations, and identifying sufficient conditions which ensure the relevant properties. Notably, we devise a general solution to the arbitrage problem , the main game-theoretic foundation behind the economic mechanisms of AMMs.

1.
Introduction

Decentralized finance (DeFi) is a software infrastructure,
based on blockchains and smart contracts,
which allows users to create and trade crypto-tokens without the
intermediation of central authorities, unlike traditional finance

[
WPG+21
,
QZA+21
]
.

Automated Market Makers (AMMs)

are one of the main DeFi archetypes:
roughly, AMMs are decentralized markets of crypto-tokens,
providing users with three core operations:
depositing crypto-tokens to obtain shares in an AMM;
the dual operation of redeeming shares in the AMM for the underlying tokens;
and
swapping tokens of a given type for tokens of another type.
The amount of tokens received by a user upon a swap is
algorithmically determined by the AMM:
roughly, this is the amount of tokens sent from the user to the AMM,
times the
swap rate
, which is computed by the AMM based on
its internal state and the input amount.

Despite the apparent simplicity of these operations,
AMMs manifest an emerging behaviour,
where users are incentivized to swap tokens
to keep their swap rates
aligned with the
exchange rate
,
i.e. the ratio between the prices of the exchanged tokens
given by external price oracles.
Namely, if an AMM offers a better swap rate than the oracles’ exchange rate,
rational users will perform swaps to narrow the gap.
Formally, the optimal strategy can be seen as the solution of
a game, called the
arbitrage game
.
Executing the optimal strategy
closes the gap between AMM’s and oracles’ exchange rates,
and in this sense AMMs offer users exhange rates
that align towards the external, global exchange rates.

As of December 2022, the two AMM platforms leading by user activity,
Uniswap and Curve Finance, alone
hold $3B and $4B worth of tokens,
and process $1B and $250M worth of transactions daily

[
uni22
,
cur22
]
.
Although this massive adoption could suggest that AMMs are a
consolidated, well-understood technology, in practice
their economic mechanisms are inherently hard to design and implement.
For instance, interactions with AMMs are sensitive to

transaction ordering attacks
,
where actors with the power to influence the
order of transactions in the blockchain can profit from
an opportunistic behaviour, causing detriment to other users.
The relevance of attacks to AMMs is witnessed by the proliferation
of scientific literature on the topic

[
BCL22
,
ZQT+21
,
DGK+20
,
EMC20
,
QZG21
]
.
Still, attacks to DeFi applications are not purely theoretical: indeed,
there is a growing history of DeFi incidents,
which have caused losses exceeding $2.4B
[
def22
]
so far.
These issues witness a need for foundational work to devise
formal theories of AMMs which allow the understanding of their
structural properties and of their economic incentive mechanisms.

Current descriptions of AMMs are either
economic models
[
AKC+21
,
AC20
,
EAC21
,
AEC20
]
,
which focus on the incentive mechanism alone,
or concrete AMM implementations.
While economic models are useful to understand
the macroscopic financial aspects of AMMs,
they do not precisely describe the interactions between AMMs and their users.
Still, a precise formalisation of these interactions is fundamental to understand the structural and economic properties of AMMs, and to determine possible deviations from safe behaviour.
Implementations, instead, reflect the exact behaviour of AMMs, but at a level of detail that hampers high-level understanding and reasoning.
Moreover, the rich variety of implementations, proposals and models for AMMs, each featuring different
economic mechanisms, makes it difficult to compare AMM designs or to provide a clear contour for the space of possible “well behaving” designs.

1.1.
Contributions

In this paper we exploit techniques from concurrency theory to provide a
formal backbone for AMMs and to study their fundamental properties.
More specifically, our
main contributions can be summarised as follows:

(1)

We introduce a formal model of AMMs (
section 2
),
which distils the common features of leading AMM implementations
like Uniswap
[
uni21
]
, Curve
[
cur21b
]
,
and Balancer
[
bal19
]
.
The core of our model is a transition system that describes
the evolution of AMM states resulting from the interaction between users and AMMs.
A peculiar aspect of our model is that it abstracts from
the
swap rate function
, a key economic mechanism of AMMs,
which is used to determine the exchange rates between tokens.

(2)

Building upon our model, in
section 3

we define basic economic notions
like token prices, exchange rates, slippage, net worth, and gain.
We compute the gain
resulting from swap actions
(Lemma
1
), and
we establish a key relation
between the gain of swap actions, the swap rate and the exchange rate:
a swap action has a strictly positive gain
if and only if

the swap rate is strictly greater than the exchange rate between
the swapped tokens
(Lemma
2
).
Both lemmata are instrumental to prove many subsequent results.

(3)

In
section 4
we establish
a set of structural properties of AMMs.
In particular, we establish preservation results for the
supply of tokens (Lemma
5
) and
for the global net worth (Lemma
7
).
We show that
assets cannot be frozen within AMMs, i.e. users can always extract
any amount of the token reserves deposited in AMMs
(Lemma
9
).
In Lemma
10
we investigate when transactions
can be reordered without affecting the resulting state.
In Theorems
11
and
12

we study compositionality of deposit and redeem transactions:
in particular, we establish that two deposit actions on the same AMM
can be merged in a single action (and similarly for two redeems),
and that the effect of deposits and redeem actions can be reverted
by suitable transactions.
Remarkably, all the structural properties in
section 4

do not depend on the choice of the swap rate function.

(4)

In
section 5
we devise sufficient conditions
on swap rate functions that induce good behavioural properties of AMMs.
These conditions allow us to extend to swap actions
the additivity and reversibility properties enjoyed by deposit and redeem
actions (Theorems
15
and
17
),
as well as to compute the gain of composed and reversed swaps
(Lemmata
16
and
18
).
We study the effect of deposits and redeems on the swap rate
and on the internal exchange rate (Lemma
20
).
We then study the properties of three notable swap rate functions:
the constant sum, the constant product, and the constant mean.

(5)

In
section 6
we investigate the incentive mechanism of AMMs.
We start by considering the
arbitrage problem
,
which requires to find the action which maximizes the gain of a user.
Performing such optimal action has the side effect of aligning the internal exchange rate of the AMM to
the external exchange rate given by token price oracles.
This gives one of the landmark economic properties of AMMs: assuming rational users, AMMs can be seen as price oracles themselves
[
AC20
]
.
Notably, while solutions to the arbitrage problem are already
known for specific swap rate functions, in Theorem
26

we generalize the result to any swap rate function respecting the
conditions given in
section 5
.
We then show that depositing tokens into AMMs incentivizes subsequent swaps
(Theorem
28
),
while redeeming tokens disincentivizes them
(Theorem
30
).
Finally, in Theorems
29
and
31

we relate the solution of the arbitrage problem in the states
before and after a deposit or redeem action, and we compare their gains.

(6)

In
section 7
we discuss Maximal Extractable Value (MEV),
a class of attacks where miners exploit their
power of dropping and reordering user transactions (and inserting their own)
to increase their gain to the detriment of users.
These attacks are one of the most carefully studied AMM phenomena,
occuring widely in practice and frequently making up the bulk of interactions with AMMs
[
QZG21
]
.
The fact that our AMM model can
accurately express these attacks
supports the coherence of our modelling choices with respect to behaviour exhibited by actual AMM implementations.

(7)

In
section 8
we discuss some extensions to our basic
AMM model to make it closer to the implementation of Uniswap
[
uni21
]
,
and their impact on the results in the paper.

(8)

As a byproduct, we provide an open-source Ocaml implementation of our
executable semantics as a companion of this paper.
1
1

1

https://github.com/blockchain-unica/defi-workbench

(9)

We provide full proofs of all our statements in the Appendices.

1.2.
Related Work

The work
[
AKC+21
]
proposed one of the first analyses
of the incentive mechanism of Uniswap.
This analysis was then generalised in
[
AC20
]
to

constant function AMMs (CFMMs)
,
where, for a pair of token types, the reserves
r_{0},r_{1}
before a swap
and the reserves
r^{\prime}_{0},r^{\prime}_{1}
after the swap
must preserve the invariant
f(r_{0},r_{1})=f(r^{\prime}_{0},r^{\prime}_{1})
,
for a given trading function
f
.
Constant product AMMs, like Uniswap, are an instance of CFMMs,
where
f(x,y)=xy
.
Both works study the arbitrage problem, for constant product AMMs
and CFMMs, respectively.
The two works show that the solution can be efficiently computed,
and suggest that constant product AMMs accurately report exchange rates.
Our work and
[
AC20
]
share a common goal,
i.e. a theory of AMMs generalizing that of constant product AMMs.
However, the two approaches are quite different.
The work
[
AC20
]
considers a class of AMMs,
i.e. CFMMs with a convex trading set,
and studies the properties enjoyed by AMMs under these assumptions.
Instead, in this paper we devise a minimal set of properties
of the swap rate function which induce good behavioural properties of AMMs.
Notably, we find conditions on the swap rate function
which ensure that a given swap action maximizes the gain of the player
(Theorem
26
).
Another difference is that the AMM model in
[
AC20
]

describes the evolution of a single AMM, abstracting away the other
components of the state (i.e. the users and the other AMMs);
instead, we model AMMs as
reactive systems
,
borrowing techniques from concurrency theory.
While the approach followed by
[
AC20
]
is still adequate
to study problems that concern AMMs in isolation (e.g., arbitrage),
viewing AMMs as reactive systems allows us to study what happens when
many agents (users and AMMs) can interact.
E.g., we are able to reason about Maximal Extractable Value
(
section 7
).

The work
[
DKP21
]
generalises the arbitrage problem
to the setting where a swap between two token types
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{n}}}

can be obtained through a sequence of
n
intermediate
swaps between
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{i}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{i+1}}}
, for
0\leq i<n
.
In practice, this represents the situation where users can interact
with different AMM platforms, each one providing its own set of token pairs.
To model this scenario,
[
DKP21
]
introduces
exchange networks
,
i.e. multi-graphs where nodes are tokens,
and edges are AMMs which allow users to swap the two endpoint tokens.
To encompass different AMM platforms, each edge has its own price function,
which determines how many output tokens
are paid for a given amount of input tokens.
The authors show that, under some conditions on the price functions
(i.e., monotonicity, continuity, boundedness and concavity),
the arbitrage problem always admits a non-trivial solution.
In the special case of constant product AMMs, a closed formula
for the solution is provided.
Besides arbitrage,
[
DKP21
]

also considers the
optimal routing problem
,
i.e. finding a strategy to maximize the amount of tokens
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}

received for at most a given amount of tokens
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
.
Under the same assumptions on the price function used for the arbitrage problem,
the optimal routing problem admits a solution.
There are several differences between our approach and that
of
[
DKP21
]
, besides the fact that we assume
the same swap rate function for all AMMs, and a graph instead of a multi-graph
(i.e., we admit at most one AMM for each token pair).
A technical difference is that we assume that the amount
y
of output
tokens received for an amount
x
of input tokens is given by

y=\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})\cdot x
, whereas
[
DKP21
]

defines this amount as
y=f_{r_{0},r_{1}}(x)
.
This results in different structural properties for
\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})

and
f(x)
in order to achieve the desired behavioural properties of AMMs.
Having the AMM reserves
r_{0}
,
r_{1}
as parameters of
our swap rate functions
\mathit{SX}
has a benefit, in that we can express
conditions which relate states before and after a transaction:
this is what happens, e.g., in the additivity, reversibility and homogeneity
properties (Definitions
5.3
,
5.4

and
5.5
).
As a consequence of this choice, compared to
[
DKP21
]

our theory encompasses also deposit and redeem actions,
providing results that clarify how these actions interfere with swaps
(e.g., Theorems
28
,
30
,
29
, and
31
).

A few alternatives to constant product AMMs have been studied.
Balancer
[
bal19
]
generalizes the constant product function
used by Uniswap to a constant (weighted geometric) mean

f(r_{1},\cdots,r_{n})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}^{w_{i}}
,
where the weight
w_{i}
reflects the relevance of a token
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{i}}}

in a tuple of tokens
({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}},\cdots,{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{n}}})
.
This still fits within the CFMM setting of
[
AC20
]
,
thus inheriting its results about solvability
of the arbitrage problem
[
EAC21
]
.
Curve
[
Ego19
]
features
a hybrid of a constant sum and constant product function,
optimized for large swap volumes between
stable coins
,
where the swap rate can support large amounts with small sensitivity.
To efficiently compute swap rates, implementations
perform numerical approximations
[
cur21a
]
.
Should these approximations fail to converge, these implementations
still guarantee that the AMM remains liquid.
The work
[
KFG21
]
proposes a
constant product invariant that is adjusted dynamically
based on the oracle price feed,
thus reducing the need for arbitrage transactions,
but at the cost of lower fee accrual.
AMMs with
virtual
balances have been proposed
[
vir18
]

and implemented
[
moo20b
,
moo20a
]
.
In these AMMs, the swap rate depends on past actions,
besides the current funds balances in the AMM.
This, similarly to
[
KFG21
]
, aims to minimize
the need for arbitrage transactions to ensure
the local AMM swap rate tends towards the exchange rates.
Establishing whether these sophisticated swap rate functions enjoy
the properties in
section 5
is an interesting open problem.

AMMs are well-known to suffer from transaction-ordering attacks,
through which an adversary with the power of influencing
the order of transactions (e.g., a miner)
can extract value from user transactions.
For instance, if the transaction pool contains
a swap transaction sent by user
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
, then a miner
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}
can extract value
from
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s swap through a transaction “sandwich” constructed as follows.
First,
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}
front-runs
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s swap with its own swap,
crafted so that
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s swap decreases
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s net worth as much as possible.
Then,
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}
closes the sandwich by appending another swap transaction
which maximizes
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}
’s gain, and finalises the whole sandwich
on the blockchain.
In this way,
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
will always have a negative gain,
which is counterbalanced by a positive gain of
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}
.
This and other kinds of attacks have fostered
the research on adversarial and defensive strategies,
and on empirical analyses of the impact of attacks

[
BCL22
,
CAE22
,
ZQT+21
,
QZG21
,
DGK+20
,
EMC20
]
.
For instance, the work
[
BCL22
]
devises an optimal strategy through
which an adversary can extract the maximal value from users’ transactions
(not only swaps, but also deposits and redeems),
in the setting of Uniswap-like AMMs.
The swap-rate-agnostic approach pursued by this paper could be exploited to
generalise the attack of
[
BCL22
]
to AMMs beyond Uniswap.

A high-level survey on various AMM protocols is in
[
XVPC22
]
.

Comparison with previous work

A preliminary version of this work was presented at
COORDINATION 2021
[
BCL21b
]
.
The current version substantially extends it,
streamlining the theory and providing additional results.
A crucial difference between the two papers is that, while in

[
BCL21b
]
the semantics of swap actions was parameterized
by an invariant between the old and the new token reserves,
here we make the semantics parametric w.r.t. the swap rate function
\mathit{SX}
.
This leads to a substantial simplification of the conditions
that are put to obtain nice behavioural properties of swaps,
and consequently of the corresponding proofs.
Among the new results w.r.t.
[
BCL21b
]
, we mention
in particular the additivity and reversibility properties
(Theorems
11
,
12
,
15
, and
17
),
and the results that relate the gain of swaps before and after
deposit/redeem actions (Theorems
28
,
30
,
29
, and
31
).
Besides these extensions, the current paper includes a discussion
of the constant sum and of the constant mean swap rate functions,
a new section on MEV attacks (see
section 7
),
and it provides detailed proofs for all its statements.

2.
A formal model of Automated Market Makers

We introduce a formal, operational model of AMMs,
which focusses on the common operations implemented by AMM platforms.
In order to simplify the resulting theory, our model abstracts from
a few features that are often found in AMM implementations,
like e.g. fees, price updates, and guarded transactions.
We discuss in §
8
how to extend our model
to make it closer to the Uniswap protocol
[
uni21
]
.

We introduce here some general notation.
We denote by
fx
the application of a function
f
to a value
x

(we use parentheses, e.g.
f(x)
, to resolve ambiguities).
We denote with
\operatorname{dom}{f}
the domain of
f
.
We use the standard notation
f\{\nicefrac{{v}}{{x}}\}

to update a partial map
f
at point
x
:
namely,
f\{\nicefrac{{v}}{{x}}\}(x)=v
, while

f\{\nicefrac{{v}}{{x}}\}(y)=fy
for
y\neq x
.

2.1.
AMM basics

Tokens

We assume a set
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{T}_{0}}}
of
atomic token types
,
which represent native cryptocurrencies and application-specific tokens.
For instance,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{T}_{0}}}
may include ETH, the native cryptocurrency
of Ethereum, and WBTC, i.e. Bitcoins wrapped with the ERC20 interface for Ethereum tokens.
A
minted token type
is an unordered pair of distinct
atomic token types:
if
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
are atomic token types
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
,
then the minted token type
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}

represents shares in an AMM holding reserves of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
.
We denote by
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{T}_{1}}}
the set of minted token types.
In our model, tokens are
fungible
,
i.e. individual units of the same type are interchangeable.
This means that amounts of tokens of the same type
can be split into smaller parts,
and two amounts of tokens of the same type can be joined.
We use
v,v^{\prime},r,r^{\prime},x,x^{\prime}
to range over
nonnegative real numbers (
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
).
We write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{T}}}
for the universe of all token types,
i.e.
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{T}}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{T}_{0}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{T}_{1}}}
,
and we use
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau^{\prime}}},\ldots
to range over
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{T}}}
.
We write
r:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}
to denote
r
units
of a token of type
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}
, either atomic or minted.

Wallets and AMMs

We assume a set of
users

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{\mathbb{A}}}}
,
ranged over by
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}},{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A^{\prime}}}},\ldots

We model the
wallet
of a user
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
as a term

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]
, where the finite partial map

\sigma\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{T}}}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

represents
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s token balance.
We model an
AMM
holding reserves of
r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and

r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
(with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
)
as an unordered pair
\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
.
Since the order of the token reserves in an AMM is immaterial,
the terms
\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}

and
\{{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}},{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}\}

denote exactly the same AMM.

States

We model the interaction between users and AMMs
as a labelled transition system (LTS).
Its labels represent blockchain
transactions
, while
the
states

\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta,\ldots
are
finite non-empty compositions of wallets and AMMs.
Formally, states are terms of the form:

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{1}}}[{\sigma_{1}}]\mid\cdots\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{n}}}[{\sigma_{n}}]\mid\{{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}},{r^{\prime}_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau^{\prime}_{1}}}}\}\mid\cdots\mid\{{r_{k}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{k}}}},{r^{\prime}_{k}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau^{\prime}_{k}}}}\}

and subject to the following conditions. For all
i\neq j
:

(1)

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{i}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{j}}

(each user has a single wallet);

(2)

\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{i}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}}\}\neq\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{j}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau^{\prime}_{j}}}\}

(distinct AMMs cannot hold exactly the same token types).

Note that these conditions allow AMMs
to have a common token type
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}
,
e.g. as in
\{{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}},{r:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}\}
,

\{{r^{\prime}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}},{r_{2}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{2}}}}\}
,
thus enabling indirect trades between token pairs
not directly provided by any AMM.
A state is
initial

when it has no AMMs, and its wallets hold only atomic tokens.
We stipulate that the ordering of terms in a state is immaterial.
Hence, we consider two states
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
to be equivalent
when they contain the same terms (regardless of their order).
For a term
Q
and a state
\Gamma
, we write
Q\in\Gamma

when
\Gamma=Q\mid\Gamma^{\prime}
, for some
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Transactions

State transitions are triggered by transactions

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}},\ldots
, which can have the following forms
(where
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
are atomic tokens):

•

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}({v_{0}}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{v_{1}:}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
.

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
deposits
v_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
v_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}

to an AMM
\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
,
receiving in return some freshly-minted units of the token
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
;

•

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({v},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
.

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
tranfers
v:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
to an AMM

\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
,
receiving in return some units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
, which are removed from the AMM;

•

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}({v:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}})
.

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
redeems
v
units of the minted token
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
:
this means that some units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
are transferred
from the AMM
\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s wallet,
and that
v
units of
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
are burned.

We denote with

\mathit{type}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})
the type of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}

(i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}
),
with
\mathit{wal}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})
the user whose wallet is affected by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
,
and with
\mathit{tok}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})
the set of token types affected by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.
For example, if

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({v},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
,
then

\mathit{type}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}
,

\mathit{wal}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})={\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
, and

\mathit{tok}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}\}
.

Token supply

We use
\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}
to denote the
supply
of a token type
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}
in a state
\Gamma
, defined
as the sum of the reserves of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}
in all the wallets and the AMMs
in
\Gamma
.
Formally, we define
\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}

by induction on the structure of states as follows:

\mathit{S}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}=\begin{cases}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}&\text{if ${\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}\in\operatorname{dom}{\sigma}$}\\
0&\text{otherwise }\end{cases}\qquad\mathit{S}_{\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}=\begin{cases}r_{i}&\text{if ${\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{i}}}$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\qquad\mathit{S}_{\Gamma\mid\Gamma^{\prime}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}=\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}+\mathit{S}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}

For example, let

\Gamma={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{1:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},2:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}]\mid\{{3:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{4:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\{{5:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{6:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{2}}}}\}
.
We have that

\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}=9
,

\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}=4
,

\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{2}}}}=6
,
while
\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}=0
for

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}\not\in\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{2}}}\}
.
Note that
\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}
is always defined, since
it is defined when
\Gamma
is an atomic term (wallet or AMM),
and states
\Gamma
are
finite
compositions of atomic terms.

2.2.
AMM semantics

We now formalise the transition rules between states.
We write
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
for a state transition
from
\Gamma
to
\Gamma^{\prime}
, triggered by a transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.
When
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
for some
\Gamma^{\prime}
, we
say that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is
enabled
in
\Gamma
.
We denote with
\xrightarrow{}^{*}
the reflexive and transitive
closure of
\xrightarrow{}
.
Given a finite sequence of transactions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{k}}
,
we write
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}}\Gamma^{\prime}

when
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\cdots\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{k}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
,
and in this case we say that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}
is enabled in
\Gamma
.
We say that a state
\Gamma
is
reachable

if
\Gamma_{0}\xrightarrow{}^{*}\Gamma
for some initial
\Gamma_{0}
.
Hereafter, all the states mentioned in our results are
implicitly assumed to be reachable.
Given a partial map
\sigma\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{T}}}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
a token type
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{T}}}
and a partial operation

\circ\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

with
\circ\in\{+,-\}
,
we define the partial map
\sigma\circ v:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}
as follows:

\sigma\circ v:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}=\begin{cases}\sigma\{\nicefrac{{(\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}})\;\circ\;v}}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}}\}&\text{if ${\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}\in\operatorname{dom}{\sigma}$ and $(\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}})\circ v\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$}\\
\sigma\{\nicefrac{{v}}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}}\}&\text{if ${\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}\not\in\operatorname{dom}{\sigma}$ and $\circ=+$}\end{cases}

These partial operations allow to increase/decrease the amount of tokens
in a balance.
For instance, if
\sigma=5:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
, then

\sigma+1:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}=6:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
, and

\sigma+1:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}=5:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},1:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
.

Deposit

Any user can create an AMM for two tokens
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
,
if such an AMM is not already present in the state.
This is achieved by the transaction

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}({v_{0}}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{v_{1}:}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
,
through which
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
transfers
v_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
v_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}

to the new AMM.
In return for the deposit,
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
receives a certain positive amount of units
of a new token type
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
,
which is minted by the AMM.
2
2

2

The actual amount of received units is irrelevant.
Here we choose
v_{0}
, but any other choice would be valid.

We formalise this behaviour by the rule:

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{i}}}\geq v_{i}>0\;\;(i\in\{0,1\})\qquad\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}=0\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{l}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]\mid\Gamma\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}({v_{0}}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{v_{1}:}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})}\\[4.0pt]
{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma-v_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}-v_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}+v_{0}:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}]\mid\{{v_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{v_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Gamma\end{array}}\;{\scriptsize\textsc{[Dep0]}}

Note that the premise
\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}=0

implies that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
are distinct atomic tokens,
since otherwise
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
would not be a minted token.
If
\Gamma
is reachable, then this premise also implies
that
\Gamma
does
not
contain an AMM
for the token pair
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
.

Once an AMM is created, any user can deposit tokens into it —

as long as
doing so preserves the ratio of the token reserves in the AMM.
When a user deposits
v_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
v_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}

to an existing AMM, it receives in return an amount of minted tokens
of type
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
.
This amount is the ratio between the deposited amount
v_{0}
and the

redeem rate
of
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}

in the current state
\Gamma
,
which is defined as follows for
i\in\{0,1\}
:

\mathit{RX}^{i}_{\Gamma}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})=\frac{r_{i}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}\qquad\text{if $\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\in\Gamma$}

(1)

The effect of a deposit transaction on the state is then
formalised by the following rule:

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l}\sigma{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{i}}}}\geq v_{i}>0\;\;(i\in\{0,1\})\qquad v_{i}=v\cdot\mathit{RX}^{i}_{\Gamma}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Gamma\;=&{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]\;\mid\;\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\;\mid\;\Delta\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}({v_{0}}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{v_{1}:}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})}\\[4.0pt]
&{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma-v_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}-v_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}+v:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}]\;\mid\;\{{r_{0}+v_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}+v_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\;\mid\;\Delta\end{array}}\;{\scriptsize\textsc{[Dep]}}

We anticipate that
the premises of the
[Dep]
rule ensure that
deposits preserve some key quantities across state transitions, namely:

•

the ratio between the reserves of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
in the AMM
(see Lemma
6
1
).
This ratio is always defined,
since the reserves of a token in an AMM cannot be zeroed
(see Lemma
4
);

•

the
net worth
of the user performing the action
(see Lemma
7
).
In particular, the value of the minted tokens
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}

received by the user upon a deposit is equal to
the value of the tokens
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
transferred to the AMM;

•

the
internal exchange rate
of the AMM
(see Lemma
19
).
This preservation property holds for a relevant class of swap rate functions,
called
homogeneous
(see Definition
5.5
).

Redeem

Any user can redeem units of a minted token
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
,
obtaining in return units of the underlying
atomic tokens
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
.
Their actual amounts are determined by the redeem rate:
the idea is that each unit of the minted token
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}

can be redeemed for equal fractions of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}

remaining in the AMM:

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l}\sigma{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}\geq v>0\qquad v<\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}\qquad v_{i}=v\cdot\mathit{RX}^{i}_{\Gamma}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})\quad(i\in\{0,1\})\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Gamma\;=&{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]\;\mid\;\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\;\mid\;\Delta\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}({v:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}})}\\[4.0pt]
&{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma+v_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}+v_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}-v:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}]\;\mid\;\{{r_{0}-v_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}-v_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\;\mid\;\Delta\end{array}}\;{\scriptsize\textsc{[Rdm]}}

Note that the premise
v<\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}

ensures that the reserves are not depleted, i.e.
v_{i}<r_{i}
.
Similarly to the
[Dep]
rule,
the premises of
[Rdm]
ensure that:

•

the net worth of the user performing the action is preserved
(i.e., the net worth of burnt minted tokens is equal to
that of the tokens received by
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
);

•

the internal exchange rate of the AMM
is unaffected by the transition,
if the swap rate function is homogeneous.

Swap

Any user
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
can swap
v
units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
in her wallet
for some units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
in an AMM

\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}

through the transaction

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({v},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
.
Symmetrically,
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
can swap
v
of her units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}

for units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
in the AMM through a transaction

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({v},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}})
.
The
swap rate

\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})

determines the amount of
output tokens

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}

that a user receives upon an amount of
x

input tokens

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}

in an AMM
\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
.

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l}\sigma{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}\geq x\qquad y=x\cdot\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})<r_{1}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{l}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]\mid\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Gamma\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})}\\[4.0pt]
{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma-x:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}+y:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid\{{r_{0}+x:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}-y:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Gamma\end{array}}{\scriptsize\textsc{[Swap]}}

The swap rate function is a parameter of our model:
we will discuss in §
5
some desiderata for this function,
and the behavioural properties they induce on the AMM semantics.
As an instance, we consider below
the
constant product swap rate

[
rva18
]
,
which is used in mainstream AMM implementations,
like e.g. in Uniswap v2
[
uni21
]
,
Mooniswap
[
moo20a
]
and SushiSwap
[
sus21
]
.
We will use this swap rate function in all the examples in this paper.

{defi}

[Constant product swap rate]

The constant product swap rate function is:

\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})\;=\;\frac{r_{1}}{r_{0}+x}

The constant product swap rate ensures that,
if an AMM
\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
evolves into

\{{r_{0}+x:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}-y:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
upon a swap,
then the product between the reserves is preserved:

(r_{0}+x)(r_{1}-y)\;=\;(r_{0}+x)\Big(r_{1}-x\cdot\frac{r_{1}}{r_{0}+x}\Big)\;=\;r_{0}r_{1}

Overall, the behaviour of the transition rules discussed above highlights
some landmark properties of AMMs, namely:

•

since neither deposits nor redeems affect the net worth of the users
performing them, the only way for users to increase their net worth
is to perform swaps.
Since, as we will see in Lemma
7
,
the
global
net worth is constant, this means that increasing ones’
net worth results in a decrease of someone else’s net worth;

•

the internal exchange rate of an AMM is affected only by swap actions
(provided that the swap rate function is homogeneous).
This is a natural behaviour, because swaps reflect the value of tokens
perceived by users.
We will show later in §
5
that
the constant sum/product/mean swap rate functions are homogeneous.

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{70:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},70:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}[{30:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},10:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}({70}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{70:}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})}\;

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{70:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}]\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}[{30:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},10:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid\{{70:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{70:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({30},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})}\;

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{70:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}]\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}[{0:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},31:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid\{{100:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{49:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({21},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}})}\;

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{70:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}]\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}[{30:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},10:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid\{{70:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{70:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}({30:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}})}\;

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{30:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},30:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}},40:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}]\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}[{30:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},10:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid\{{40:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{40:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({30},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})}\;

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{30:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},30:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}},40:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}]\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}[{0:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},27:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid\{{70:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{23:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}({30:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}})}\;

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{82:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},47:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}},10:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}]\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}[{0:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},27:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid\{{18:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{6:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}

Figure 1.
Interactions between two users and an AMM.

{exa}

Figure

1
shows a computation in our model.
We discuss below the effect of the fired transactions, showing
in
Figure

2
the evolution of the token reserves in the AMM:

(1)

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}({70}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{70:}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
.
Starting from an initial state,

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
creates a new AMM, depositing

70:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
70:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
.
In return,
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
receives
70
units of the minted token

\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
.

(2)

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({30},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
.

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}
swaps
30
units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
for an
amount
y
of units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
determined by the swap rate.
Since we are assuming the constant product swap rate,
we obtain
y=30\cdot\nicefrac{{70}}{{70+30}}=21
.
This swap rate function ensures that swaps preserve
the product between the token reserves in the AMM:
in
Figure

2
, we show indeed that the swap
results in a traversal along the curve
k=70\cdot 70
from

\{{70:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{70:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
to

\{{100:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{49:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
.

(3)

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({21},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}})
.

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}
reverses the effect of his previous action
by swapping
21
units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}

for
y=21\cdot\nicefrac{{100}}{{49+21}}=30
of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
.

Figure

2
shows that the swap results
in a traversal from

\{{100:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{49:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
to

\{{70:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{70:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
along the curve
k=70\cdot 70
.

(4)

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}({30:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}})
.

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}
redeems
30
units of the minted token
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
,
accordingly reducing the token reserves in the AMM to

\{{40:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{40:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
.
Note that the received tokens exhibit the same 1-to-1 ratio as after
the initial deposit.

(5)

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({30},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
.

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}
swaps
30
units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
, receiving

y=30\cdot\nicefrac{{40}}{{40+30}}\approx 17
units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
.
Note that the swap rate, i.e.
\nicefrac{{40}}{{40+30}}\approx 0.57
,
has decreased w.r.t. the first swap, i.e.
\nicefrac{{70}}{{70+30}}=0.7
,
even though the AMM has the same 1-to-1 reserves ratio.
This is caused by the reduction in reserves occurred after
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s
redeem action: thus, the swap rate is sensitive not only to
the ratio of reserves in the AMM, but also on their actual values.

(6)

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}({30:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}})
.

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
redeems
30
units of the minted token

\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
, thereby extracting
52
units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}

and
17
units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
from the AMM.
Note that the ratio of redeemed tokens is no longer 1-to-1 as
in the previous redeem action,
as the prior swap has changed the ratio between the
funds of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
in the AMM.

Finally, observe that the supply of both
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}

remains constant.
We will show in Lemma
5

that the supply of atomic token types is always preserved.

\diamond

Figure 2.
Evolution of reserves of AMM
({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})
along the trace in
Figure

1
.

3.
Prices, exchange rates and net worth

In this
section
we introduce some economic notions
which are pivotal for understanding the economic mechanisms of AMMs.

Token prices and exchange rates

We assume an external oracle that prices atomic tokens.
Formally, we model this oracle as a function

P:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{T}_{0}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
assuming that the prices given by the oracle
are constant along executions
(see
subsection 8.2
for a discussion about
dynamic price updates).
While the prices of atomic tokens are constant,
that of minted tokens may vary at run-time
as a function of the state.
More precisely, the price
P_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}

of a minted token
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
depends both
on the supply of the minted token in the users’ wallets
and on the reserves of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
in the AMM:

P_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}\;=\;\dfrac{r_{0}\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}+r_{1}\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}\mbox{\qquad}\text{if $\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\in\Gamma$}

(2)

For uniformity, we define
P_{\Gamma}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}=P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}

when
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{T}_{0}}}
.
Lemma
4
will ensure that

r_{0},r_{1}>0
and

\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}>0

in every reachable state
\Gamma
containing an AMM
for the token pair
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
.
Therefore, the price of the token
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}

is always defined and positive in reachable states.

The idea underlying
Equation

2
is that
the price of one unit of minted token must be
equal to the value of the atomic tokens
that can be obtained by redeeming the minted token.
Indeed, by rule
[Rdm]
and
Equation

1
we have that:

P_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}\;=\;\dfrac{r_{0}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}+\dfrac{r_{1}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}=\mathit{RX}^{0}_{\Gamma}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}+\mathit{RX}^{1}_{\Gamma}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}

which substantiates our desideratum.
This intuition will be formalized later in Lemma
8
.

The
exchange rate

X({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})

between atomic token types
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}

is the number of units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}

that one can buy with
1
unit of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
at the price given by the external oracle:

X({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\;=\;\frac{P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}}{P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}}

(3)

Hence, assuming an exchange at the prices of the external oracle,
a user paying
x
units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
would receive

x\cdot X({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
.

Note that the exchange rate between two token types only depends on external
oracles, neglecting the state of AMMs.
However, AMMs themselves can act as (decentralised)
price oracles
[
AC20
]
,
since they induce an exchange rate
based on the effect of swaps in the current state.
More precisely, the
internal exchange rate

X_{\Gamma}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})

between two atomic token types
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}

in a state
\Gamma
is
the limit of the swap rate function as
x
approaches
0
:

3
3

3

This notion is also dubbed as marginal price
[
AC20
]
or spot exchange rate
[
XVPC22
]
in literature.

X_{\Gamma}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\;=\;\lim_{x\rightarrow 0}\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})\qquad\text{if $\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}$}

(4)

The intuition is similar to that in
Equation

3
:
a user swapping
x
units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
for
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
through the AMM
(for
x

very small
) would expect to receive

x\cdot X_{\Gamma}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
.
We will see later in
section 6

that rational users will perform actions
that align the internal exchange rate to the one given by external oracles.

Slippage
measures the discrepancy between the internal exchange rate
and the actual ratio between the amounts of output and input tokens
obtained upon the swap
[
XVPC22
]
:

\Delta X_{\Gamma}({x,{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\;=\;\frac{X_{\Gamma}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})}{\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})}-1\qquad\text{if $\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}$}

(5)

Ideally, slippage should disadvantage large trades, i.e. trying to obtain a larger amount of tokens with a swap
should make them more expensive, increasing the slippage.
We will compute in sections
5.6
-
5.8

the internal exchange rate and the slippage of some common AMMs.

{exa}

Let

\Gamma={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{82:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},47:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}},10:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}]\mid\{{18:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{6:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}[{\cdots}]

be the final state of the computation in
Figure

1
.
We have that
\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}=10
,
since only
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s wallet contains units of the minted token.
Assume that the prices of atomic tokens are

P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}=5
and
P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}=9
.
The price of the minted token
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
is then:

P_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}=\frac{18\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}+6\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}}{10}=\frac{18\cdot 5+6\cdot 9}{10}=14.4

The exchange rate between the two tokens is:

X({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\;=\;\frac{P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}}{P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}}\;=\;\frac{5}{9}\;=\;0.55

which means that to buy
1
unit of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
,
one needs
0.55
units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
.
Note instead that the internal exchange rate is:

X_{\Gamma}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\;=\;\lim_{x\rightarrow 0}\mathit{SX}({x,18,6})=\frac{6}{18}\approx 0.33

We will see in Example
6.1
that
the discrepancy between internal and oracle exchange rate
can by exploited by users to increase their gain.
The slippage of a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
is:

\Delta X_{\Gamma}({x,{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\;=\;\frac{X_{\Gamma}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})}{\mathit{SX}({x,18,6})}-1\;=\;\frac{x}{18}

from which we can see that the slippage grows with the input amount
x
.

\diamond

Net worth and gain

The
net worth
of a user
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
is a measure of
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s
wealth in tokens (both atomic and minted).
Formally, we define the net worth of
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
in a state
\Gamma
as:

W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma)\;=\;\begin{cases}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}\in\operatorname{dom}{\sigma}}\sigma{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}\cdot P_{\Gamma}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}&\text{if ${{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]\in\Gamma$}\\[4.0pt]
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(6)

Note that
W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma)\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, since
balances
\sigma
are
finite
maps,
and
P_{\Gamma}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}
is always defined.

The
global net worth

W(\Gamma)
in a state
\Gamma

is the sum of the net worth in users’ wallets.
Note that the token reserves in AMMs are not accounted for by
W(\Gamma)
,
because their value is already recorded by minted tokens
held in users’ wallets.
Indeed, the equality:

\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}\cdot P_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}\;=\;r_{0}\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}+r_{1}\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}

between the net worth of a minted token and the value of the AMM
is a direct consequence of the definition of price in Equation (
2
).

We denote by
\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}})
the
gain
of user
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}

upon performing a sequence of transactions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}
enabled in state
\Gamma

(if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}
is not enabled in
\Gamma
, we stipulate that the gain is zero):

\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}})\;=\;W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})-W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma)\qquad\text{if $\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}}\Gamma^{\prime}$}

(7)

To maximize their gain, users can perform
different interactions with the AMM,
e.g., by investing tokens or trading units of differently priced token types.

The following lemma quantifies
the gain of users upon firing a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}
transaction.
Note that this quantification does not depend on any of the
properties of the swap rate function introduced later on in
section 5
:
actually, it holds for any swap rate function.

Lemma 1

(Swap gain)
.

Let
\Gamma=\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Delta
,
and let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})

be enabled in
\Gamma
.
Then:

\displaystyle\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})

\displaystyle=\phantom{-}x\cdot\big(\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})\,P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}-P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}\big)\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}\Big)

if
{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}}]\in\Gamma

\displaystyle\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})

\displaystyle=-x\cdot\big(\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}-P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}\big)\cdot\frac{\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}

if
{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}[{\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}}]\in\Gamma
,
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}

A direct consequence of Lemma
1
is that
if
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
performs a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}
between
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}

and she holds all the units of the minted token
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
,
then her gain will be zero.
Further,
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
maximizes her gain when she has no minted tokens
of type
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
.
The lemma also implies that if the user performing the swap
has a positive gain, then all the users who hold units of

\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
will have a negative gain.

The following lemma states that a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}
transaction on an AMM

\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}

has a strictly positive gain
if and only if

the swap rate is strictly greater than the oracle exchange rate between

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
.
This holds for
any
swap rate function, under the condition that
the user who performs the swap has no minted tokens
of type
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
.

Lemma 2

(Swap rate
vs.
exchange rate)
.

Let
\Gamma={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]\mid\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Delta

be such that
\sigma{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}=0
, and
let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})

be enabled in
\Gamma
.
Then:

\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})\circ 0\iff\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})\circ X({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})

{exa}

Let

\Gamma_{0}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{70:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},70:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}[{30:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},10:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]

be the initial state of the computation in
Figure

1
.
Let
P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}=5
and
P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}=9
.
The users’ net worth in
\Gamma_{0}
and in the final state

\Gamma={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{82:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},47:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}},10:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}]\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}[{0:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},27:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid\{{18:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{6:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}

is as follows:

\displaystyle W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma_{0})=70\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}+70\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}=980

\displaystyle W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}(\Gamma_{0})=30\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}+10\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}=240

\displaystyle W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma)=82\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}+47\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}+10\cdot P_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}=977

\displaystyle W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}(\Gamma)=27\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}=243

Note that
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s net worth of has decreased w.r.t. the initial state, while that of
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}
has increased:
indeed, the gain of
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
upon the sequence of transactions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}
is

\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}})=977-980=-3
,
while that of
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}
is

\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}})=243-240=3
.
One may think that
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}
has been more successful than
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
,
but this depends on the users’ goals.
Note, e.g., that
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
holds
10
units of the minted token

\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
,
whose price may increase in the future.
\diamond

4.
Structural properties of AMMs

We now establish some structural properties of AMMs,
which do not depend on the design of the economic mechanisms,
i.e. on the choice of the swap rate function.
These structural properties are the basis for AMM interactions
that occur in the wild, and that cumulatively give rise to
complex emerging behaviours like arbitrage and MEV.
Hence, establishing these structural properties is
a preliminary sanity check for our AMM model.
We will provide further support for the coherence between our model
and actual AMMs by showing that the above-mentioned complex behaviours
are expressible in our model (see
section 6
and
section 7
).

First, we establish that the AMMs’ transition system is deterministic.
This follows from the fact that,
given a state
\Gamma
and a transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
,
there is at most one applicable rule.
Note that determinism is a crucial property for blockchains,
since it ensures that all the nodes in the blockchain network
are able to reconstruct a common state from a sequence of transactions.
Therefore, it makes sense that determinism holds also for our AMM model.

Lemma 3

(Determinism)
.

If
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\Gamma^{\prime}

and
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
, then

\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
.

We can lift the statement to sequences of transactions
by using a simple inductive argument.
The same applies to other single-step results in this
section
.

Lemma
4
ensures
that the reserves in an AMM cannot be zeroed,
and that the same holds for the units of any minted token.
Summing up, this ensures that the price of any minted token
is always defined and positive.

Lemma 4

(Non depletion)
.

For all states
\Gamma
,
if
\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\in\Gamma

then:

(1)

r_{i}>0
, for
i\in\{0,1\}
;

(2)

\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}>0
.

4.1.
Preservation properties

Lemma
5
ensures that transactions preserve
the supply of
atomic
tokens.
Minted tokens, instead, are preserved only by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}
transactions,
since deposit and redeem transactions, respectively,
create and destroy minted tokens.
This fact will be instrumental to prove the preservation of
the net worth (see Lemma
7
).

Lemma 5

(Preservation of token supply)
.

Let
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Then:

(1)

for all
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{T}_{0}}}
,

\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}=\mathit{S}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}

(2)

if
\mathit{type}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}
, then for all
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{T}_{1}}}
,

\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}=\mathit{S}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}

Lemma
6
states
that deposit and redeem transactions preserve
the reserves ratio in AMMs, the redeem rate, and the price of minted tokens.
These preservation properties will be exploited later on to
determine the solution to the arbitrage game after deposits and redeems
(see Theorems
29
and
31
).

Lemma 6

(Preservation upon deposits/redeems)
.

Let
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
, with

\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\in\Gamma
.
If
\mathit{type}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})\in\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}\}
, then:

(1)

if

\{{r^{\prime}_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r^{\prime}_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
,
then

\nicefrac{{r_{1}}}{{r_{0}}}=\nicefrac{{r^{\prime}_{1}}}{{r^{\prime}_{0}}}

(2)

\mathit{RX}^{i}_{\Gamma}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})=\mathit{RX}^{i}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})
,
for
i\in\{0,1\}

(3)

P_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}=P_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}

Lemma
7
ensures that transactions
(of any type) preserve the
global
net worth,
whereas the net worth of individual users is preserved
only by redeem and deposit transactions.
A direct consequence of this preservation result
is that users can increase their net worth only by performing swaps:
Indeed, we will find in Theorem
26
that the solution
of the arbitrage game only contains
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}
transactions.
Furthermore, if a user has a positive gain, then some other user
must have a loss.

Lemma 7

(Preservation of net worth)
.

Let
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Then:

(1)

if
\mathit{type}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}

then, for all
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
:
W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma)=W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})

(2)

W(\Gamma)=W(\Gamma^{\prime})

The following lemma, which is a direct consequence of
Lemma
7
1
,
supports the definition of the price of minted tokens in
Equation

2
:
indeed, computing the net worth of a user
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
under that price definition
corresponds to making
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
first redeem all her minted tokens, and then
summing the price of the resulting atomic tokens.

Lemma 8
.

Let
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
,
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}
contains only
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}
actions of
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
.
If
{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]\in\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\operatorname{dom}{\sigma}\cap{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{T}_{1}}}=\emptyset
,
then:

W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma)\;=\;\sum_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}\in\operatorname{dom}{\sigma}}\sigma{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}

{exa}

Let

\Gamma={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{82:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},47:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}},10:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}]\mid\{{27:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{9:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}[{5:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}]
,
and let
P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}=5
and
P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}=9
.
We have that
P_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}=14.4
,
and
W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma)=977
.
Assume that
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
performs a transaction from
\Gamma

to redeem all
10
units of
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
in her wallet.
The resulting state is

\Gamma^{\prime}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{100:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},53:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid\{{9:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{3:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\cdots
.
We compute
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s net worth in
\Gamma^{\prime}
using the oracle token prices:

W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})\;=\;100\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}+53\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\;=\;100\cdot 5+53\cdot 9\;=\;977

which is coherent with the net worth predicted by Lemma
8
.

\diamond

4.2.
Liquidity

Lemma
9
ensures that funds cannot be
frozen
in an AMM,
i.e. that users can always redeem arbitrary amounts of the tokens deposited
in an AMM, as long as the reserves are not zeroed.
Note that, since

\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}=\{{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}},{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}\}
,
the statement also holds when swapping
r_{0}
with
r_{1}
.

Lemma 9

(Liquidity)
.

Let
\Gamma
be such that

\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\in\Gamma
.
Then, for all
r^{\prime}_{0}<r_{0}
,
there exist
r^{\prime}_{1}<r_{1}
,

\Gamma^{\prime}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}
only containing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}
transactions such that

\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}}\{{r^{\prime}_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r^{\prime}_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Gamma^{\prime}
.

4.3.
Reordering of transactions

In general, given two transactions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
and a state
\Gamma
,
executing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
from
\Gamma

yields different states.
However, under some conditions it is possible to invert the order
of the two transactions, preserving the resulting state.
This is always the case, e.g., of two transactions which operate on
disjoint sets of tokens.
Lemma
10
establishes sufficient conditions
for preserving the state upon reordering.
Besides the case cited before, this is always possible if
both transactions are deposits, or if they are bot redeems
(case
1
of the statement).
Note that, in these cases, the assumption that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
is enabled
in
\Gamma
implies that also
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
is such.
This is no longer true when one of the two transactions is a deposit
and the other one is a redeem.
For instance, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
redeems the minted tokens obtained upon a deposit
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
may not be enabled in
\Gamma
because there are not
enough minted tokens in the user’s wallet.
Therefore, case
2
of the statement uses the additional
hypothesis that also
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
is enabled in
\Gamma
.

Lemma 10

(Reordering of transactions)
.

Let
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\Gamma_{01}
.
Then:

(1)

if
\mathit{tok}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}})\cap\mathit{tok}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}})=\emptyset

or
\mathit{type}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}})=\mathit{type}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}})\in\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}\}
,
then
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\Gamma_{01}
;

(2)

otherwise, if
\mathit{type}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}),\mathit{type}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}})\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}

and
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\Gamma_{10}
,
then
\Gamma_{01}=\Gamma_{10}
.

As we shall see in
section 6
,
it is actually desirable, and crucial for the economic mechanism of AMMs,
that swaps interfere with other transactions
that trade the same token type.

4.4.
Additivity of deposit and redeem actions

Deposit and redeem actions satisfy an additivity property:
if a user performs two successive deposits (resp. redeems) on an AMM,
then the same result can be obtained through a single deposit (resp. redeem).
Instead, swap actions are not additive, in general:
we will study sufficient conditions for the additivity
of swap actions in
section 5
(see Theorem
15
).

Theorem 11

(Additivity)
.

Let
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\Gamma_{0}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\Gamma_{1}
.
Then:

(1)

if

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}({v_{0}}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{v_{1}:}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})

and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}({v^{\prime}_{0}}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{v^{\prime}_{1}:}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
,
then:
