\Gamma\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}({v_{0}+v^{\prime}_{0}}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{v^{\prime}_{1}+v^{\prime}_{1}:}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})}\Gamma_{1}

(2)

if

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}({v:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}})

and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}({v^{\prime}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}})
,
then:

\Gamma\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}({v+v^{\prime}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}})}\Gamma_{1}

4.5.
Reversibility of deposit and redeem actions

The following theorem establishes that
deposit and redeem transactions are
reversible
:
more precisely,
the effect of a deposit action can be reverted by a redeem action,
and
vice versa
,
the effect of a redeem action can be reverted by a deposit action.
The only exception is a deposit action that creates an AMM,
through the rule
[Dep0]
.
Swap actions are not reversible, in general:
we will study sufficient conditions for their reversibility
in
section 5
(see Theorem
17
).

Theorem 12

(Reversibility)
.

Let
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
,
where
\mathit{type}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})\in\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}\}
and
for all
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{T}_{1}}}
,
if
\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}=0
then
\mathit{S}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}=0
.
Then there exists
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}^{-1}
such that

\Gamma^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}^{-1}}\Gamma
.

In general, the study of reversible computation models,
which dates back to
[
Ben73
]
,
is an active area of research,
which has led to a wide range of applications in software systems
[
MSG+20
]
.
In particular, the reversibility of AMM actions has useful consequences
on their behaviour.
For instance, it guarantees that,
starting from a “stable” state where no arbitrage is possible,
after any transaction it is possible to return to the stable state.
More in general, if the swap rate function satisfies the conditions
of
section 5
that ensure the additivity and reversibility
also for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}
actions, then for
any sequence of transactions:

\Gamma_{0}\;\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\;\Gamma_{1}\;\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}\;\cdots\Gamma_{n}

it is possible to fire another transaction and return
to the state
\Gamma_{0}
.
Indeed, by additivity we obtain that the effect of the sequence

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{n}}
can be emulated by a single transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
,
and then reversibility ensures that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
can be reversed, i.e.:

\Gamma_{0}\;\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\;\Gamma_{n}\;\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}^{-1}}\;\Gamma_{0}

5.
The swap rate function

In the previous section we have established some key structural properties
of deposit and redeem actions, e.g. their additivity and reversibility.
In general, these properties do not hold for swap actions:
it is easy to find swap rate functions
\mathit{SX}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

that make these properties false.
Throughout this section we introduce some general properties
of swap rate functions,
and we discuss the properties they induce on the behaviour of AMMs.
In sections
5.6
-
5.8

we then discuss the properties enjoyed by the swap rate functions
used in some concrete AMM implementations.
Coherently with these implementations,
we assume that a swap rate function is defined and non-negative for all
x>0
,
and that the internal exchange rate
(i.e., the limit of
\mathit{SX}
for
x
leading to
0
, see (
4
))
is always defined.

5.1.
Output-boundedness

Output boundedness guarantees that an AMM has always enough output tokens

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
to send to the user who performs a

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
.

{defi}

[Output-boundedness]

A swap rate function
\mathit{SX}
is
output-bounded
when,
for all
x,r_{0},r_{1}
such that

x\geq 0
and
r_{0},r_{1}>0
:

x\cdot\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})<r_{1}

The following lemma establishes sufficient conditions
for a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}
action to be enabled.

Lemma 13
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
, and
let
{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]\in\Gamma
.
If
\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}>0
,

\sigma({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}})\geq x
and

\mathit{SX}
is output-bounded, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is enabled in
\Gamma
.

5.2.
Monotonicity

Consider a transaction

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})

on an AMM
\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
.
Without making any assumptions on the swap rate function,
there is no relation between the effect of this transaction
and that of a swap where the parameters have been varied.
Monotonicity, instead, ensures that there exists a meaninful relation:
the swap rate increases if
we decrease the input amount
x
or the reserves of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
,
and if we increase the reserves of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
.
The intuition is that
lower reserves of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
in the AMM
make the
x:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
paid by
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
more “valuable” for the AMM,
hence the AMM will output more units of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
for the same input amount.
Increasing the reserves of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
in the AMM
(keeping those of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
unaltered) produces the same effect.
Monotonicity on
x
also ensures that
the internal exchange rate of the AMM is defined, for each token pair.

{defi}

[Monotonicity]

A swap rate function
\mathit{SX}
is
monotonic
when:

x^{\prime}\leq x,\;r^{\prime}_{0}\leq r_{0},\;r_{1}\leq r^{\prime}_{1}\implies\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})\leq\mathit{SX}({x^{\prime},r^{\prime}_{0},r^{\prime}_{1}})

Further,
\mathit{SX}
is
strictly monotonic
when,
for
i\in\{0,1,2\}
and
\lhd_{i}\in\{<,\leq\}
:

x^{\prime}\lhd_{0}x,\;r^{\prime}_{0}\lhd_{1}r_{0},\;r_{1}\lhd_{2}r^{\prime}_{1}\implies\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})\lhd_{3}\mathit{SX}({x^{\prime},r^{\prime}_{0},r^{\prime}_{1}})

where:

\lhd_{3}=\begin{cases}\leq&\text{if $\lhd_{i}=\,\leq$ for $i\in\{0,1,2\}$}\\
<&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Note that strict monotonicity trivially implies monotonicity.
The following lemma relates monotonicity of the swap rate function
with the gain of swap transactions,
concretising the intuition given before from the point of view of
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s gain.

Lemma 14
.

Let
\Gamma=\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Delta

and
\Gamma^{\prime}=\{{r^{\prime}_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r^{\prime}_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Delta
,
with
r^{\prime}_{0}\leq r_{0}
and
r_{1}\leq r^{\prime}_{1}
,
and let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})

be enabled in
\Gamma
and in
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
If
\mathit{SX}
is monotonic, then

\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})\leq\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma^{\prime}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})
.

5.3.
Additivity

To extend the additivity property of Theorem
11

to swap actions, we must require that the swap rate function is additive.

{defi}

[Additivity]

A swap rate function
\mathit{SX}
is
additive
when:

\alpha=\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}}),\;\beta=\mathit{SX}({y,r_{0}+x,r_{1}-\alpha x})\implies\mathit{SX}({x+y,r_{0},r_{1}})=\frac{\alpha x+\beta y}{x+y}

The idea here is that a user fires a swap transaction (say,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
)
with input amount
x
in a state
\Gamma
,
and then in the state reached after firing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
,
she fires another swap transaction (say,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
)
with input amount
y
on the same AMM.
The definition of additivity requires that the swap rate
of a swap transaction with input amount
x+y
in
\Gamma

is in a given relation with
the swap rates computed for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}

and with the input amounts
x
and
y
.
Theorem
15
states that if this relation holds,
then a single swap with input amount
x+y
in
\Gamma
produces exactly
the same effect of performing first
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
and then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
.
Then, Lemma
16
allows us to compute
the gain of this transaction as the sum of the gains of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
.

Theorem 15

(Additivity of swap)
.

Let
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\Gamma_{0}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\Gamma_{1}
,
with

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{i}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x_{i}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})

for
i\in\{0,1\}
.
If
\mathit{SX}
is additive, then:

\Gamma\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x_{0}+x_{1}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})}\Gamma_{1}

Lemma 16

(Additivity of swap gain)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}(x)={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
,
and let
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}(x_{0})}\Gamma^{\prime}
.
If
\mathit{SX}
is output-bounded and additive, then:

\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}(x_{0}+x_{1})})\;=\;\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}(x_{0})})+\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma^{\prime}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}(x_{1})})

5.4.
Reversibility

The reversibility property in Theorem
12

states that the effect of deposit and redeem transactions can be reverted.
We now devise a property of swap rate functions that give the same
guarantee for swap transactions.

{defi}

[Reversibility]

A swap rate function
\mathit{SX}
is
reversible
when:

\alpha=\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})\implies\mathit{SX}({\alpha x,r_{1}-\alpha x,r_{0}+x})\;=\;\frac{1}{\alpha}

Consider now a state

\Gamma=\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Delta
,
and let
\alpha=\lim_{x\rightarrow 0}\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})

be the internal exchange between
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
in
\Gamma
.
If the swap rate function is reversible, then:

\lim_{x\rightarrow 0}\mathit{SX}({x,r_{1},r_{0}})\;=\;\lim_{x\rightarrow 0}\mathit{SX}({\alpha x,r_{1}-\alpha x,r_{0}+x})\;=\;\lim_{x\rightarrow 0}\,\frac{1}{\alpha}\;=\;\frac{1}{\alpha}

from which we obtain:

X_{\Gamma}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}})\;=\;\frac{1}{X_{\Gamma}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})}

(8)

The intuition of Definition
5.4
is that,
to reverse the effect of a swap transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}

that pays
x:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
to receive
y:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
,
one must fire a swap transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}^{-1}

that pays
y:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
to receive
x:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
.
Of course, this results in the same AMM state that we had before performing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.
Writing
\alpha
for the swap rate
\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})
,
the
[Swap]
rule fixes
y=\alpha x
.
Hence, assuming that in the initial state the AMM has reserves

r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
, after performing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}

its reserves will be
r_{0}+x:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and

r_{1}-\alpha x:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
.
In this state, requiring that the swap rate for an input of

y:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
is
\tfrac{1}{\alpha}

(as done by Definition
5.4
)
implies that the AMM outputs
x:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
,
reverting the reserves of the AMM to the initial values.

The following theorem formalises the intuition above, establishing that, when
the swap rate function is reversible,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}
transactions are reversible.
Together with Theorem
12
,
all the AMM actions are reversible under this hypothesis.

Theorem 17

(Reversibility of swap)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
, and
let
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
.
If
\mathit{SX}
is reversible,
then there exists
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}^{-1}
such that

\Gamma^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}^{-1}}\Gamma
.

Lemma
18
allows us to compute the
gain of the reverse transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}^{-1}
in the state
reached after performing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}

as a function of the gain of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.
As expected by preservation of the global net worth,
the gain of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}^{-1}
is the opposite of that of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.

Lemma 18
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
, and
let
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
.
If
\mathit{SX}
is reversible, then

\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})=-\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma^{\prime}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}^{-1}})
.

5.5.
Homogeneity

A swap rate function is homogeneous when the swap rate
is not affected by a linear scaling of the three parameters.
Homogeneity is useful to relate the swap rate
before and after deposit or redeem transactions, since their
effect is a linear scaling of the AMM reserves.
Lemma
19
establishes one the the landmark
properties of AMMs we have anticipated in
section 2
:
when the swap rate function is homogeneous,
deposits and redeems do not affect the internal swap rate.

{defi}

[Homogeneity]

A swap rate function
\mathit{SX}
is
homogeneous
when,
for
a>0
:

\mathit{SX}({ax,ar_{0},ar_{1}})\;=\;\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})

Lemma 19

(Preservation of internal exchange rate upon deposits/redeems)
.

Let
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
,
where

\mathit{tok}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}\}

and

\mathit{type}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})\in\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}\}
.
If
\mathit{SX}
is homogeneous, then:

X_{\Gamma}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})=X_{\Gamma^{\prime}}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})

The following lemma shows that deposits increase swap rates,
whilst redeems have the opposite effect.
Dually, deposits decrease the slippage, while redeems increase it.
In
section 6
we will exploit this fact to show that
deposits incentivize swaps, while redeems disincentivize them
(see Theorems
28
and
30
).

Lemma 20
.

Let
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
,
where

\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\in\Gamma
,

\{{r^{\prime}_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r^{\prime}_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\in\Gamma^{\prime}

and
\mathit{tok}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}\}
.
If
\mathit{SX}
is homogeneous and strictly monotonic, then for all
x>0
:

(1)

\mathit{type}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}\,\implies\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})<\mathit{SX}({x,r^{\prime}_{0},r^{\prime}_{1}})\text{ and }\Delta X_{\Gamma}({x,{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})>\Delta X_{\Gamma^{\prime}}({x,{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})

(2)

\mathit{type}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}\implies\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})>\mathit{SX}({x,r^{\prime}_{0},r^{\prime}_{1}})\text{ and }\Delta X_{\Gamma}({x,{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})<\Delta X_{\Gamma^{\prime}}({x,{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})

It is easy to find swap rate functions that violate
the properties discussed before:
for instance
\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})=\nicefrac{{1}}{{x}}

violates output-boundedness, additivity, reversibility and homogeneity.
In the rest of the section we discuss some notable swap rate functions,
used in actual AMM implementations, showing that they
satisfy most of our properties.

5.6.
Constant sum swap rate

The
constant sum
function
mandates the sum of the token reserves in an AMM to remain constant,
i.e.
r_{0}+r_{1}=k
, where the constant
k
is fixed
upon the first deposit in the AMM.

Theorem 21

(Constant sum swap rate)
.

The
constant sum
swap rate function:

\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})\;=\;1

is
monotonic,
reversible,
additive,
and homogeneous.
Furthermore,
its internal swap rate and its slippage are given by:

X_{\Gamma}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\;=\;1\qquad\qquad\Delta X_{\Gamma}({x,{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\;=\;0

Note that the constant sum function is
not
output-bounded,
since the output amount may exceed the reserves of the output token.
A positive aspect of constant sum AMMs
is that they do not suffer from slippage.
With constant sum AMMs, the internal exchange rate is always
1
,
and so there is zero slippage (see
Equations

4
and
5
).
A negative aspect is that constant sum AMMs do not allow
the token reserves to grow unboundedly:
indeed, the bound is fixed with the first deposit.
This makes constant sum AMMs unsuitable for scenarios where
one wants the liquidity of the AMM to increase over time,
and to incentivise users to deposit through minted tokens.
When the oracle and internal exchange rates are not aligned
(i.e., when the prices of the two tokens are different), then
rational users will drain the reserves
of the most expensive token type held by the AMM.
Despite these drawbacks, the constant sum swap rate is suitable situations
where the two token types in the AMM are supposed to be equally prices,
like for stablecoins.
This is the case e.g. for mStable
[
mSt20
]
.

5.7.
Constant product swap rate

The constant product swap rate function
(introduced before in Definition
2.2
)
enjoys all the properties discussed previously in this section.
4
4

4

The existence of other classes of swap rate functions enjoying all the six properties is an open question.

Theorem 22

(Constant product)
.

The constant product swap rate function is
output-bounded,
strictly monotonic,
reversible,
additive, and
homogeneous.
Furthermore,
its internal swap rate and its slippage are given by:

X_{\Gamma}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\;=\;\frac{r_{1}}{r_{0}}\qquad\qquad\Delta X_{\Gamma}({x,{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\;=\;\frac{x}{r_{0}}

Compared to the constant sum swap rate, a point in favour of the
constant product is output-boundedness,
which allows users to add unbounded liquidity to the AMM.
A point against is slippage, which grows linearly with the amount
of the input token.
Therefore, when the internal exchange rate is aligned with the oracle’s,
users are disincentivised from performing large swaps.
The most prominent AMM platform adopting the constant product
is Uniswap v2
[
uni21
]
.
Curve
[
cur20
]
uses a hybrid swap rate function,
which approximates a constant sum for an interval of input values
x
, and
behaves as a constant product outside the interval.
In this way, it achieves a small slippage within the interval,
at the same time allowing unbounded liquidity thanks to output-boundedness.

5.8.
Constant mean swap rate

The constant mean swap rate function generalises the constant product
by associating weights
w_{0},w_{1}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
to the token types held by the AMM,
so to preserve the following equality:

r_{0}^{w_{0}}r_{1}^{w_{1}}=(r_{0}+x)^{w_{0}}(r_{1}+y)^{w_{1}}\qquad\text{where }y=x\cdot\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})

The following theorem shows that the constant mean function
enjoys most of the properties of the constant product, except reversibility.

Theorem 23

(Constant mean swap rate)
.

The
constant mean
swap rate function:

\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})\;=\;\frac{r_{1}}{x}\bigg(1-\Big(\frac{r_{0}}{r_{0}+x}\Big)^{\frac{w_{0}}{w_{1}}}\bigg)

is
output-bounded,
monotonic,
additive,
and homogeneous.
Furthermore,
its internal swap rate and its slippage are given by:

X_{\Gamma}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\;=\;\frac{r_{1}w_{0}}{r_{0}w_{1}}\qquad\qquad\Delta X_{\Gamma}({x,{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\;=\;\frac{xw_{0}}{r_{0}w_{1}\Big(1-\big(\frac{r_{0}}{r_{0}+x}\big)^{\frac{w_{0}}{w_{1}}}\Big)}-1

The most prominent AMM plaform using the constant mean swap rate
is Balancer
[
bal19
]
.
Users fix the weights
w_{0},w_{1}
of token types when an AMM is created;
once fixed, these weights cannot be changed.
The constant product swap rate can be seen as the special case of the constant
mean where the two weights are equal.

6.
The economic mechanism of AMMs

AMMs can be seen as games where users compete to increase their net worth.
We now study the incentive mechanisms of AMMs from
a game-theoretic perspective.

6.1.
Arbitrage

The
arbitrage game
is a single-player, single-round game,
where the player can perform a single move on a given AMM pair

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
in order to maximize her gain.
The initial game states have the form

\Gamma_{0}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]\mid\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Delta
,
where
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
is the player;
the
moves
of
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
are all the possible transactions
that can be fired by
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}

(we also consider doing nothing as a possible move).
More formally, a move is a sequence
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}
such that
either
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}=\varepsilon
(the empty sequence),
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
with
\mathit{wal}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})={\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
.
The goal of
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
is to maximize her gain

\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma_{0}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}})

on the AMM pair
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
.
A
solution
to the game is a move
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}
that satisfies such goal.
We study the arbitrage game under the assumption
that
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
holds no minted tokens
\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
.
In this way, by Lemma
1
,

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s gain only depends on
the input amount of
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s swap,
on the reserves of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
in the AMM,
and on their prices.
In practice, AMM users are logically partitioned in two groups,
e.g. liquidity providers (who perform deposits and redeems) and traders
(who perform swaps), so basically here we are considering the
arbitrage game from the traders’ point of view.
We further assume that
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s balance is enough to allow
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}

to perform the optimal swap.
This is a common assumption in formulations of the arbitrage game:
in practice, this can be achieved by borrowing the needed amount of
the input token from a lending pool via
a flash-loan
[
QZLG21
,
WWL+20
]
.
Theorem
26
shows that
a rational agent is incentivized to perform a swap
to realign the internal and the oracle’s exchange rate.
The optimal solution to the arbitrage game can be approximated
by multiple users who swap smaller amounts than the optimal one.

Before devising a solution to the arbitrage game,
we examine the potential candidates for the solution.
Observe that doing nothing (i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}=\varepsilon
) has clearly zero gain,
as well as depositing or redeeming,
as established by Lemma
7
.
Hence, if one of such moves is a solution, so are the other two:
without loss of generality, we assume that
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s move will be

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}=\varepsilon
when there is no strategy which allows

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
to increase her gain.

We first show in Lemma
25
that,
if a swap with input
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and output
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
has a positive gain,
then a swap with input
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
and output
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}

will have a negative gain, whatever input amount is chosen.
This holds whenever the swap rate function is monotonic and reversible.
Lemma
24
is instrumental to prove Lemma
25
,
as it finds the needed relation between the swap rate function and the exchange rate.
Passing from this relation to the gain of the swap transaction
is obtained by means of Lemma
2
.

Lemma 24
.

If
\mathit{SX}
is strictly monotonic and reversible, then for all
x>0
:

\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})\geq X({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\implies\forall y>0.\;\mathit{SX}({y,r_{1},r_{0}})<X({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}})

Lemma 25

(Unique direction for swap gain)
.

Let

\Gamma={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]\mid\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Delta

be such that
\sigma{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}=0
, and
let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{d}}(x)={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{d}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1-d}}}})
,
for
x>0
and
d\in\{0,1\}
.
If
\mathit{SX}
is output-bounded, strictly monotonic and reversible, then
for all
y>0
such that
\sigma{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1-d}}}}\geq y
:

\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{d}}(x)})>0\implies\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1-d}}(y)})<0

Theorem
26

devises a general solution to the arbitrage game,
determining the swap transaction that maximizes
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s gain.
This is the transaction

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x_{0}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})

such that, in the state
\Gamma^{\prime}
reached after performing it
from the initial state,
the internal exchange rate between
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}

is aligned to the oracle’s exchange rate.
By Lemma
2
,
no move from
\Gamma^{\prime}
can increase
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s gain,
i.e. the solution for the arbitrage game in
\Gamma^{\prime}
is to do nothing.
Lemma
25
guarantees that
swaps in the other direction are not solutions,
since they decrease
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s gain.
Note that
if the internal exchange rate is already aligned to the oracle’s,
or if
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
has not enough balance to perform the optimal swap,
then the solution to the arbitrage problem is to do nothing.

Theorem 26

(Arbitrage)
.

Let
\Gamma={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]\mid\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Delta

be such that
\sigma{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}=0
.
For all
x>0
,
let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}(x)={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
.
Let
x_{0}
be such that:

X_{\Gamma^{\prime}}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})=X({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\qquad\text{ where }\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}(x_{0})}\Gamma^{\prime}

(9)

If
\mathit{SX}
is output-bounded, strictly monotonic, additive and reversible, then:

\forall x\neq x_{0}\;:\;\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}(x_{0})})\;>\;\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}(x)})

Furthermore, if an
x_{0}
satisfying
Equation

9
exists,
it is unique.

An implicit desideratum on these solutions is that,
given a specific instance of the swap rate function,
they are efficiently computable:
this is the case, e.g., for the constant product,
for which Lemma
27
finds a closed formula
for the arbitrage solution.

Lemma 27

(Arbitrage and constant product)
.

Let
\Gamma={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]\mid\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
,
and let:

x_{0}\;=\;\sqrt{\frac{P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}}{P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}}r_{0}r_{1}}-r_{0}

(10)

If
\mathit{SX}
is the constant product swap rate and
x_{0}>0
,
then
{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x_{0}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})

is the solution to the arbitrage game in
\Gamma
.

{exa}

Consider an initial state

\Gamma={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]\mid\{{18:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{6:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Delta
.
Assuming the constant product swap rate,
and
P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}=3
,
P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}=4
,
we have that:

\displaystyle X_{\Gamma}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\;=\;6/18\;<\;3/4\;=\;X({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})

\displaystyle X_{\Gamma}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}})\;=\;18/6\;>\;4/3\;=\;X({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}})

By Theorem
26
it follows that
the solution to the arbitrage game is

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}(x)={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}})
,
for suitable
x
.
By Lemma
27
, we find that
the optimal input value is:

x_{1}\;=\;\sqrt{\frac{3}{4}\cdot 18\cdot 6}-6=3

and the corresponding output value is

x_{1}\cdot\mathit{SX}({x_{1},6,18})=6
.
We then obtain:

\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}(x_{1})}\Gamma^{\prime}\;=\;{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma-3:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}+6:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}]\mid\{{12:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{9:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}

This action maximizes
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s gain

\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}(x_{1})})=W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})-W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma)=6P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}-3P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}=6
.
Any other action would result in a lower gain for
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
.
Note that the internal exchange rate in
\Gamma^{\prime}
is aligned to the oracle’s:

X_{\Gamma^{\prime}}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})=9/12=3/4=X({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
.

\diamond

6.2.
Swaps after deposits

We show in Theorem
28
that deposits incentivise swaps.
Namely, if a user
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}
performs a deposit on an AMM for the token pair

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
,
and then a
different
user
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
performs a swap in the resulting state,
then
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s gain is increased w.r.t. the gain that she would have
obtained by performing the same transaction
before

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}
’s deposit.
The intuition is that larger amounts of tokens in an AMM
provide decrease the slippage, therefore attracting users interested in swaps.

Theorem 28

(Swap after deposit)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}}}
be two transactions
such that

\mathit{wal}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}}}})={\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}\neq\mathit{wal}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}}}})
and,
for
\ell\in\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}\}
,

\mathit{type}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\ell}}})=\ell
and

\mathit{tok}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\ell}}})=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}\}
.
Let
\Gamma
be such that both

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}}}

are enabled in
\Gamma
.
If the swap rate function is homogeneous and strictly monotonic, then:

\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}}}})>\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}}}})

{exa}

Let
\Gamma={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{5:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}]\mid\{{5:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{10:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Delta
,
let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}({40}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{80:}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
,
and let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({5},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
.
Assuming the constant product swap rate,
we have that:

\displaystyle\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}}}}\Gamma_{s}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{5:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid\{{10:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{5:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Delta

\displaystyle\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}}}}\Gamma_{d}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{5:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}]\mid\{{45:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{90:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Delta^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}}}}\Gamma_{ds}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{9:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid\{{50:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{81:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Delta^{\prime}

Now, assuming
P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}=1
and
P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}=1
,
we have the following gains for
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
:

\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}}}})\;=\;4\;>\;0\;=\;\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}}}})

as correctly predicted by Theorem
28
.
Note that in the state
\Gamma
before the deposit,

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
has zero gain from her swap,
while the same transaction has a positive gain after the deposit.

\diamond

Theorem
29
finds
the solution of the arbitrage game after a deposit of another user.
More precisely, let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}
be the solution in
\Gamma
,
and let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda^{\prime}}}
be the solution in the state
\Gamma^{\prime}

reached after a deposit. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}
is empty, then also
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda^{\prime}}}
is such.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}
is a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}
with input
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
and output
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
,
then also
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda^{\prime}}}
is such (but for the input amount).

Theorem 29

(Arbitrage after deposit)
.

Let
\Gamma={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]\mid\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Delta
,
and let:

\Gamma\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}({v_{0}}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{v_{1}:}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})}\Gamma_{d}\qquad\text{where $\Gamma_{d}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma^{\prime}}]\mid\{{r^{\prime}_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r^{\prime}_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Delta^{\prime}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}$}

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda_{d}}}
be the solutions of the arbitrage game
in
\Gamma
and in
\Gamma_{d}
, respectively.
If
\mathit{SX}
is output-bounded, strictly monotonic, additive, reversible,
and homogeneous, then:

(1)

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda_{d}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({ax},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\qquad\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma_{d}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda_{d}}}})=a\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}})\qquad\text{where }\;a=\frac{r_{1}+v_{1}}{r_{1}}

(2)

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}=\varepsilon
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda_{d}}}=\varepsilon
.

6.3.
Swaps after redeems

We now study swaps and arbitrage after redeems.
Conversely to what we have shown before in Theorem
28
,
we find that redeems disincentivise swaps
(Theorem
30
).
Similarly to Theorem
29
,
if the solution to the arbitrage game in a state
\Gamma
is a swap,
then after a redeem in
\Gamma
the solution is still a swap
which only differs in the input amount
(Theorem
31
).

Theorem 30

(Swap after redeem)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}}}
be two transactions
such that

\mathit{wal}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}}}})={\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}\neq\mathit{wal}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}}}})
and,
for
\ell\in\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}\}
,

\mathit{type}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\ell}}})=\ell
and

\mathit{tok}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\ell}}})=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}\}
.
Let
\Gamma
be such that both

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}}}

are enabled in
\Gamma
.
If the swap rate function is homogeneous and strictly monotonic, then:

\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}}}})<\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}}}})

Theorem 31

(Arbitrage after redeem)
.

Let
\Gamma={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]\mid\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Delta
,
and let:

\Gamma\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}({v:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}})}\Gamma_{d}\qquad\text{where $\Gamma_{d}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma^{\prime}}]\mid\{{r^{\prime}_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r^{\prime}_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Delta^{\prime}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}$}

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda_{d}}}
be the solutions of the arbitrage game
in
\Gamma
and in
\Gamma_{d}
, respectively.
If
\mathit{SX}
is output-bounded, strictly monotonic, additive, reversible,
and homogeneous, then:

(1)

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda_{d}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({ax},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\qquad\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma_{d}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda_{d}}}})=a\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}})\qquad\text{where }\;a=1-\frac{v}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}

(2)

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}=\varepsilon
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda_{d}}}=\varepsilon
.

7.
Maximal extractable value

Maximal Extractable Value (MEV) refers to a class of attacks to smart contracts
where miners/validators exploit their power to reorder, drop or insert transactions in a block
to “extract” value from the
mempool

(i.e., the set of transactions sent to the blockchain network, but not appearing yet in a block).
Empirical research has shown that AMMs
are routinely targeted by MEV attacks
[
DGK+20
,
QZG21
,
ZQC+21
,
ZQT+21
]
,
and indeed recent versions of the Ethereum protocol implementation
include a MEV extraction mechanism
[
mev22
]
.
This has negative effects on AMM users, as well as on transaction fees
and network congestion.

We show that our AMM model makes it possible to faithfully express MEV attacks.
Consider a constant product AMM for two token types
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
with the same price, e.g.
P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}=P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}=1
, and
consider a state:

\Gamma\;=\;{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}}[{\cdots}]\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{50:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}]\mid\{{10:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{10:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\cdots

where we use
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
to impersonate a honest user,
and
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}
for a miner, acting as an adversary.
By Lemma
2
we know that the AMM is in equilibrium
in
\Gamma
, because, for each
x>0
:

\mathit{SX}({x,10,10})=\frac{10}{10+x}<1=X({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})

Therefore, neither a miner nor any other user
can increase their net worth in
\Gamma
.

Assume now that
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
sends the transaction

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({50},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})

to the blockchain network.
Before being included in a block,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}}
is added to the mempool,
from where miners gather transactions to construct blocks.
Any miner owning enough token units can increase their gain by firing

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s transaction within a
sandwich
of
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}
’s swaps.
For instance, assume that
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}
’s wallet is

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}}[{40:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},1:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]
.
Then
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}
can construct a block:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}\;=\;{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({40},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\;\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}}\;\;{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({9},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}})

We have that
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
, where:

\displaystyle\Gamma\;

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({40},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}}[{0:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},9:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{50:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}]\mid\{{50:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{2:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\cdots

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({50},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})\,}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}}[{0:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},9:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{0:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},1:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid\{{100:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{1:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\cdots

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({9},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}})\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}}[{90:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},0:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{0:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},1:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid\{{10:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{10:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\cdots\;=\Gamma^{\prime}

This results in a positive gain for
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}
, since:

\displaystyle\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}})

\displaystyle=W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})-W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}}(\Gamma)=90\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}-(40\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}+1\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})=49

\displaystyle\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}})

\displaystyle=W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})-W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma)=1\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}-50\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}=-49

Summing up,
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}
has managed to extract value from
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s transaction in the mempool,
improving her gain to the detriment of
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s net worth.

The mechanism of
guarded transactions
,
which allows users to specify a lower bound to the amount of tokens
outputted upon a swap (see
section 8
),
is a partial countermeasure against MEV attacks.
For instance, in the scenario above

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
could have sent a guarded transaction

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({50}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{8.3}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
,
which would have ensured
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
to receive at least
8.3:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
upon the swap.
This would have neutralised the sandwich attack described before,
since after the first
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{M}}}
’s transaction,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}}}}}
is no longer valid.
Even though guarded transactions mitigate the issue of not knowing the state
where one’s transaction will be fired,
they are not a complete defence against MEV attacks.
Indeed, in
[
BCL22
]
it is shown that
adversaries can craft sandwiches
that extract value from
any
non-empty mempool
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}
(guarded) transactions.
Further analyses the effect of MEV on constant-function AMMs
are developed in
[
KDC22
]
.
Several approaches to prevent MEV attacks are discussed
in
[
HW22
,
ByCD+21
]
.

8.
Variants of the basic model

Our AMM model abstracts from implementation-specific features,
and from the features that are orthogonal
to the core functionality of AMMs (e.g., governance).
We discuss below some extensions and variants of our model to make it closer to actual
implementations, and their impact on our theory.

8.1.
Fees

In actual AMM implementations,
the swap rate — and consequently, the semantics of
[Swap]
actions —
also depends on a
trading fee

1-\phi
.
For instance, incorporating this fee in the
constant product swap rate function is usually done as follows:

\mathit{SX}_{\phi}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})\;=\;\frac{\phi\,r_{1}}{r_{0}+\phi\,x}\qquad\text{where }\phi\in[0,1]

In this case, when the trading fee is zero (i.e.,
\phi=1
),
the swap rate preserves the product between AMM reserves;
a higher fee, instead, results in reduced amounts of output tokens
received from swap actions.
Intuitively, the AMM retains a portion of the swapped amounts,
but the overall reserves are still distributed among all minted tokens,
thereby increasing the redeem rate of minted tokens.
The structural properties in
section 4
are
not affected by swap fees.

8.2.
Price updates

An underlying assumption of our model is that the price of atomic tokens
is constant, and consequently that exchange rates are stable.
In the wild, prices and exchange rates can vary over time,
possibly making the net worth of users holding minted tokens decrease —
a phenomenon commonly referred to as
impermanent loss

[
imp20
]
.

Introducing price updates in our AMM model is straightforward:
it suffices to extend states
\Gamma
with price oracles,
parameterize with
\Gamma
the exchange rate
X
,
and extend the AMM semantics with a rule to non-deterministically
update token prices.
Most of the structural properties in
section 4

would not be affected by this extension: the exceptions are
determinism (Lemma
3
) and
net worth preservation
(Lemma
7
2
,
while part
1
would still be true for deposits
and redeems).
Technically, also the properties about swaps and incentives
in
section 5
and
section 6
are preserved,
although this happens because most of these properties
assume sequences of deposits, redeems and swaps.
If we allow these actions to be interleaved with price updates, some
properties no longer hold:
notably, the optimality of the solution
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}
to the arbitrage problem
(Theorem
26
)
is lost if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\lambda}}
is front-run by a price update that
alters the exchange rates, since this affects the condition
provided by Theorem
26
.

In practice, the assumption of constant exchange rates
assumed by Theorem
26
may hold
in the case of exchanges between stable coins
[
mak20
]
.
Here, arbitrage ensures the alignment between swap rates and
exchange rates, so users are hence incentivized to provide liquidity to AMMs,
as the redeem rate is likely to increase over time.

8.3.
Guarded transactions

The semantics of AMMs in
section 2
defines how the state evolves
upon transactions.
In practice, when a user emits a transaction,
she cannot predict the exact state in which it will be actually committed.
This may lead to unexpected or unwanted behaviours.
For instance, the gain of a swap transaction sent by
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}

may be reduced if the transaction is front-run by a redeem transaction
sent by
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}
, as established by Theorem
30
.
The problem here is that redeems decrease the swap rate
(by Lemma
20
),
and consequently the amount of output tokens received by
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
.
As a partial countermeasure to this issue, Uniswap allows users to
specify a lower bound
y^{\mathit{min}}
to the amount of received tokens.
In our model, we could formalise this behaviour by amending
the
[Swap]
rule as follows:

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l}\sigma{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}\geq x>0\qquad y=x\cdot\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})\qquad y^{\textit{min}}\leq y<r_{1}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{l}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]\mid\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Gamma\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}({x}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{y^{\textit{min}}}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})}\\[4.0pt]
{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma-x:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}+y:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}]\mid\{{r_{0}+x:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}-y:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Gamma\end{array}}{\scriptsize\textsc{[Swap]}}

Similar countermeasures apply to
[Rdm]
and
[Dep]
rules.
For redeems, the user can enforce lower bounds

v_{0}^{\mathit{min}}
,
v_{1}^{\mathit{min}}
on the amount
of received tokens
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}
as follows:

\dfrac{\sigma{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}\geq v>0\qquad v<\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}\qquad v_{i}=v\cdot\mathit{RX}^{i}_{\Gamma}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})\qquad v_{i}^{\textit{min}}\leq v_{i}}{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Gamma\;=&{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]\;\mid\;\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\;\mid\;\Gamma^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}({v:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\},v_{0}^{\textit{min}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},v_{1}^{\textit{min}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})}\\[4.0pt]
&{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma+v_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}+v_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}-v:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}]\;\mid\;\{{r_{0}-v_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}-v_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\;\mid\;\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}}{\scriptsize\textsc{[Rdm]}}

Amending the
[Dep]
rule is more complex, since here
we must define ranges for the deposited amounts
v_{0}
,
v_{1}
,
and we must preserve the ratio between the AMM reserves.
A possible way to achieve this behaviour is the following rule:

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l}\sigma{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{i}}}}\geq v_{i}>0\quad v=\frac{v_{i}}{\mathit{RX}^{i}_{\Gamma}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})}\quad(v_{0},v_{1})=\begin{cases}(v_{0}^{\textit{max}},v_{0}^{\textit{max}}\cdot\frac{r_{1}}{r_{0}})&\text{if $v_{1}^{\textit{min}}\leq v_{0}^{\textit{max}}\cdot\frac{r_{1}}{r_{0}}\leq v_{1}^{\textit{max}}$}\\
(v_{1}^{\textit{max}}\cdot\frac{r_{0}}{r_{1}},v_{1}^{\textit{max}})&\text{if $v_{0}^{\textit{min}}\leq v_{1}^{\textit{max}}\cdot\frac{r_{0}}{r_{1}}\leq v_{0}^{\textit{max}}$}\\
\end{cases}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Gamma\;=&{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma}]\;\mid\;\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\;\mid\;\Gamma^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}({v_{0}^{\textit{min}},v_{0}^{\textit{max}}}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{v_{1}^{\textit{min}},v_{1}^{\textit{max}}}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})}\\[4.0pt]
&{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}[{\sigma-v_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}-v_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}+v:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}]\;\mid\;\{{r_{0}+v_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}+v_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\;\mid\;\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}}{\scriptsize\textsc{[Dep]}}

These amendments,
which are coherent with Uniswap implementation
[
uni21
]
,
preserve all the properties, both structural and economic,
established in the previous sections,
modulo a restatement of the properties which have transactions in their
hypotheses.
For instance, in Theorem
29
,
the scaling factor
a
will be computed on the actual deposited value,
rather than on the parameter of the transaction.
Note that, although the new rules can disable some transactions
which were enabled with the rules in
section 2
,
this does not affect the transactions reordering result
(Lemma
10
).

8.4.
Other variants

There are further differences between our model and the existing AMM platforms,
that could be accounted for in extensions of our model.
Uniswap implements flash-loans as part of the swap actions:
namely, the user can optionally borrow available pair funds
[
uni20a
]

whilst returning these within the same
atomic group
of actions.
Further, Uniswap implements an exchange rate oracle, allowing
smart contracts to interpret (averages of) recent swap rates
as exchange rates
[
uni20b
]
.
Balancer
[
bal19
]
extends token pairs to token
tuples
:
a user can swap any two non-coinciding sets of supported tokens,
such that the swap rate is maintained.
In all AMM implementations, token balances are represented as integers:
consequently, they are subject to rounding errors
[
rva18
]
.
AMM platforms frequently implement a governance logic,
which allow “governance token” holders to coordinate changes
to AMM fee-rates or swap rate parameters.

9.
Conclusions

We have proposed a theory of AMMs,
which encompasses and generalizes the main
functional and economic aspects of the mainstream AMM implementations,
providing solid grounds for the design of future AMMs.

The core of our theory is a formal model of AMMs (
section 2
),
based on a thorough inspection of leading AMM implementations
like Uniswap
[
uni21
]
, Curve
[
cur21b
]
,
and Balancer
[
bal19
]
.
An original aspect of our model is that
it is parametric with respect to the key economic mechanism
— the
swap rate function
—
that algorithmically determines exchange rates between tokens.
Our model features an
executable semantics
,
which can support future implementations and analysis tools;
an open-source implementation of our semantics is available as a companion of this paper.

Building upon our model, we prove a set of properties characterizing
both structural (
section 4
)
and economic (
section 3
,
section 6
) aspects of AMMs.
Structural properties include, e.g.,
that value cannot be created or destroyed (Lemma
7
),
that tokens cannot be frozen within an AMM (Lemma
9
)
and that some sequences of transactions can be reordered without
affecting their semantics (Lemma
10
).
Concerning the economic properties, we address the
arbitrage problem
,
the main game-theoretic foundation behind the economic incentives of AMMs.
Theorem
26
provides sufficient conditions
for the existence of solutions,
and links the solutions to the expected relation between
internal exchange rate and oracle’s exchange rate.
We show that deposits incentivize swaps,
while redeems have the opposite effect.
With respect to previous works, which focus on specific economic mechanisms,
all our results are parametric with respect to the swap rate function.
We identify indeed, for each property, a set of conditions
on swap rate functions that are sufficient for the property to hold
(
section 5
).

AMM platforms like Uniswap
[
uni21
]
and Curve
[
Ego19
]

have overtaken centralized cryptocurrency markets in size and usage.
On the one hand, a better understanding of AMM design
in cases where AMMs host the majority of the token’s global swap volume
is critical
[
AEC20
]
.
On the other hand, the growth of AMMs is making them more attractive
for malicious users, even if it is difficult to exactly quantify
the effect of attacks.

This paper, together with our work on formalizing another DeFi archetype
called
lending pool

[
BCL21a
]
,
is the first step towards a general theory of DeFi
[
BCL21c
]
.
We believe that a general theory encompassing interactions
between different DeFi archetypes is crucial
to be able to reason about their structural, economic and security aspects,
as typical DeFi applications operate within a wider ecosystem,
composed by a set of collaborating or competing agents,
which interact through possibly separate execution environments.

Appendix A
Proofs for Section
3

Proof of Lemma
1

Let
\Gamma
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
be as in the hypotheses,
let
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
, and
let
y=x\cdot\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})
.
By definition of gain (Equation
7
), we have that:

\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})\;=\;W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})-W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma)

By definition of net worth (Equation
6
), we have that:

\displaystyle W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}})\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}\;+\;\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}

\displaystyle+\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\cdot\dfrac{r_{0}\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}+r_{1}\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}

\displaystyle+\textstyle\sum_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}\not\in\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}},\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\}}\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}})\cdot P_{\Gamma}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}

\displaystyle W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}})-x)\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}\;+\;(\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})+y)\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}

\displaystyle+\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\cdot\dfrac{(r_{0}+x)\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}+(r_{1}-y)\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}

\displaystyle+\textstyle\sum_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}\not\in\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}},\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\}}\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}})\cdot P_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}

Since

\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}=\mathit{S}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}

and

P_{\Gamma}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}=P_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}}

for all
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau}}\neq\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
:

\displaystyle W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})-W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle=y\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}-x\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}+\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\frac{x\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}-y\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}

\displaystyle=\big(y\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}-x\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}\big)\Big(1-\frac{\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}\Big)

\displaystyle=x\cdot\big(\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})\,P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}-P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}\big)\Big(1-\frac{\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}\Big)

Using similar calculations, for
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\frac{x\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}-y\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}

∎

Proof of Lemma
2

Let
y=x\cdot\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})
.
Since
\sigma{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}=0
,
by Lemma
1
we have that:

\displaystyle\mathit{G}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}({\Gamma},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})\circ 0\iff

\displaystyle y\ P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}-x\ P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}\circ 0

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\frac{y}{x}\circ\frac{P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}}{P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\mathit{SX}({x,r_{0},r_{1}})\circ X({{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})

∎

Appendix B
Proofs for Section
4

Proof of Lemma
3

Straightforward inspection of the rules

[Dep0]
,
[Dep]
,
[Rdm]
,
[Swap]
in
section 2
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4

For item
1
,
we proceed by induction on the length of a computation

\Gamma_{0}\xrightarrow{}^{*}\Gamma
, where
\Gamma_{0}
is initial.
The base case (computation of zero steps) is trivial,
since initial states does not contain AMMs. For the inductive case,
note that rule
[Dep0]
requires that the initial reserves
of an AMM are strictly greater than zero.
The rules that decrease the token reserves in AMMs,
i.e.
[Rdm]
and
[Swap]
, have premises that ensure
that the reserves cannot be zeroed.

For item
2
,
we proceed by induction on the length of a computation

\Gamma_{0}\xrightarrow{}^{*}\Gamma
, where
\Gamma_{0}
is initial.
The base case is trivial, since initial states do not contain AMMs.
For the inductive case,
we assume that
\Gamma
satisfies the property,
and we prove that it is preserved
by a transition
\Gamma\xrightarrow{}\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Assume that
\Gamma^{\prime}
contains an AMM

\{{r^{\prime}_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r^{\prime}_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}
.
By item (
1
),

r^{\prime}_{0}>0
and
r^{\prime}_{1}>0
.
There are the following cases, depending on the rule used to
infer
\Gamma\xrightarrow{}\Gamma^{\prime}
:

•

[Dep0]
,
[Dep]
.
Trivial, because deposits can only increase the supply of minted tokens.

•

[Swap]
.
Trivial, because
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf swap}}
actions do not affect the supply of minted tokens.

•

[Rdm]
.
Assume that
\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\in\Gamma
.
By contradiction, suppose that the
[Rdm]
action burns
all the supply of the minted token, i.e. it burns

v=\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}
units.
The rule premise requires
v>0
, and it implies:

r^{\prime}_{0}\;=\;r_{0}-v\frac{r_{0}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}\;=\;0\qquad\qquad r^{\prime}_{1}\;=\;r_{1}-v\frac{r_{1}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}\;=\;0

Therefore, we would have
r^{\prime}_{0}=r^{\prime}_{1}=0
— contradiction. ∎

Proof of Lemma
5

By cases on the rule used in the transition

\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
.
It is straightforward to check that, in all the rules,
the changes applied to atomic tokens cancel out.
Further, the
[Swap]
rule does not affect the supply
of minted tokens.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6

Let
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
,
where
\{{r_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\in\Gamma

and
\{{r^{\prime}_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{r^{\prime}_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau^{\prime}_{1}}}}\}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}({v_{0}}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{v_{1}:}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
,
then by the
[Dep]
rule it must be

r^{\prime}_{i}=r_{i}+v_{i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
.
Furthermore, by the premises of
[Dep]
, we obtain:

r_{1}v_{0}=r_{1}v\cdot\mathit{RX}^{0}_{\Gamma}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})=v\cdot\frac{r_{0}r_{1}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}=r_{0}v\cdot\mathit{RX}^{1}_{\Gamma}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})=r_{0}v_{1}

Therefore:

\frac{r_{1}+v_{1}}{r_{0}+v_{0}}\;=\;\frac{(\frac{r_{0}v_{1}}{v_{0}})+v_{1}}{r_{0}+v_{0}}\;=\;\frac{r_{0}v_{1}+v_{0}v_{1}}{(r_{0}+v_{0})v_{0}}\;=\;\frac{(r_{0}+v_{0})v_{1}}{(r_{0}+v_{0})v_{0}}\;=\;\frac{v_{1}}{v_{0}}\;=\;\frac{r_{1}}{r_{0}}

(11)

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}({v:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}})
,
then by rule
[Rdm]
it must be, for
i\in\{0,1\}
:

r^{\prime}_{i}\;=\;r_{i}-v_{i}\;=\;r_{i}-v\mathit{RX}^{i}_{\Gamma}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})\;=\;r_{i}-v\frac{r_{i}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}

Therefore, since
\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}=\mathit{S}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}+v
:

\frac{r_{1}-v_{1}}{r_{0}-v_{0}}\;=\;\frac{r_{1}-v\frac{r_{1}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}}{r_{0}-v\frac{r_{0}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}}\;=\;\frac{r_{1}(\mathit{S}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}+v)-vr_{1}}{r_{0}(\mathit{S}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}+v)-vr_{0}}\;=\;\frac{r_{1}}{r_{0}}

(12)

Summing up, (
11
) and (
12
)
give item
1
.

For item
2
,
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}=

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}({v_{0}}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{v_{1}:}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
,
then by the
[Dep]
rule it must be

r^{\prime}_{i}=r_{i}+v_{i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
,
and
\mathit{S}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}=\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}+\frac{v_{i}}{r_{i}}\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}
.
Therefore:

\mathit{RX}^{i}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})=\frac{r_{i}+v_{i}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}=\frac{r_{i}+v_{i}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}(1+\frac{v_{i}}{r_{i}})}=\frac{(r_{i}+v_{i})r_{i}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}(r_{i}+v_{i})}=\mathit{RX}^{i}_{\Gamma}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})

Otherwise, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}({v:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}})
,
then by rule
[Rdm]
it must be, for
i\in\{0,1\}
:

r^{\prime}_{i}\;=\;r_{i}-v_{i}\;=\;r_{i}-v\mathit{RX}^{i}_{\Gamma}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})\;=\;r_{i}-v\frac{r_{i}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}

Therefore:

\displaystyle\mathit{RX}^{i}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{i}-v_{i}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}-v}=\frac{r_{i}-v\frac{r_{i}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}-v}=\frac{r_{i}\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}-vr_{i}}{(\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}-v)\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{i}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}=\mathit{RX}^{i}_{\Gamma}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}})

∎

For item
3
,
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}({v_{0}}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{v_{1}:}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
,
we have that:

\displaystyle P_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{r^{\prime}_{0}\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}+r^{\prime}_{1}\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}

by
Equation

2

\displaystyle=\dfrac{(1+\frac{v_{0}}{r_{0}})\cdot r_{0}\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}+(1+\frac{v_{1}}{r_{1}})\cdot r_{1}\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}}{\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}+\frac{v_{i}}{r_{i}}\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{(1+\frac{v_{i}}{r_{i}})\cdot r_{0}\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}+(1+\frac{v_{i}}{r_{i}})\cdot r_{1}\cdot P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}}{\big(1+\frac{v_{i}}{r_{i}}\big)\cdot\mathit{S}_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}}

since
\tfrac{v_{0}}{r_{0}}=\tfrac{v_{1}}{r_{1}}

\displaystyle=P_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}

by
Equation

2

The proof for the case

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf rdm}}({v:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}})

is similar.

Proof of Lemma
7

Let
\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
.
We first prove
item

1
.
Depending on the rule used to fire the transition,
we have the following cases:

•

[Dep0]
.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf dep}}({v_{0}}:{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{v_{1}:}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}})
.
We have that:

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}[{\sigma}]\mid\Gamma_{0}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}}[{\sigma-v_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}-v_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}+v_{0}:\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}]\mid\{{v_{0}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{v_{1}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}\mid\Gamma_{0}

If
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
’s net worth is unaffected.
Otherwise, if
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{B}}}={\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}
, then:

\displaystyle W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})

\displaystyle=W_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\sf{A}}}}(\Gamma)-v_{0}P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}}-v_{1}P{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}+v_{0}P_{\Gamma}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{0}}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\tau_{1}}}}\}}
